- This is a linear programming (LP) model that schedules or sizes activities in a chemical reaction / material-flow network so that material requirements are satisfied while an objective is minimized (typically cost, waste, or penalty). It is intended for process engineers or planners who must decide reaction or flow levels subject to technical limits.

- The model has 34 decision variables (named y[1]..y[34] in the stored model). These represent controllable quantities such as reaction rates, process flows, or activity levels of units. (A stored example value: y[1] = 0.0.)

3) What information it uses (parameters / data)
- A parameter named rm is present and appears to encode stoichiometry or resource mapping (how each reaction affects each material). rm contains 55 indexed entries (example: rm['01',4,1] = 1).
- Other typical data (not fully listed in the short summary) are likely present: cost coefficients used in the objective, material demand/availability, and capacity/limit numbers for reactions.

4) Constraints (what rules the decisions must follow)
- material_cons: 15 material-balance constraints. These enforce conservation or demand satisfaction for each material (total in − total out = required amount or similar).
- reaction_cons: 22 reaction-related constraints. These set reaction capacities, technical linkages, or other limits on reaction usage.

5) Objective (what the model minimizes)
- A single minimization objective labeled obj. The stored summary shows an objective value of 1.0 for the solved instance, so the model was solved and returned that objective value.

6) Model type and reported solution status
- Model type: Linear program (LP).
- Reported solution snapshot (from stored summary): objective = 1.0, 34 decision variables present, constraints grouped as above. The summary includes only one explicit variable example (y[1] = 0.0); the full vector of variable values and dual values were not included in the brief summary.

7) How to interpret results (practical takeaways)
- The solution tells you which reaction/flow levels achieve material requirements at minimum objective value given the data and constraints.
- Active vs inactive reactions: variables with zero value are inactive; positive values indicate selected/used reactions or flows.
- Dual/price information (not shown in the short summary) would tell you marginal value of relaxing material constraints (useful to identify bottlenecks).

# Load base model and modify availability of chemical 5 (hydronium ion)
from pyomo.environ import Constraint

# Load model
model = load_model('chem', models_dictionary)

# Add dual suffix to capture duals where available (LP parts); note: duals not defined for MIPs
model = add_dual_suffix(model)

# Deactivate original availability constraint that forced y[5] == 1
model.material_cons[5].deactivate()

# Add new constraint to set availability of chemical 5 to zero
model.material_cons_5_override = Constraint(expr=model.y[5] == 0)

# Solve the modified model
description = (
    "What-if analysis: Set availability of chemical 5 (hydronium ion) to zero by deactivating "
    "material_cons[5] (which originally enforced y[5]==1) and adding material_cons_5_override: y[5]==0. "
    "Objective is to re-evaluate acetone feasibility (y[6]) and the induced reaction logic under this change."
)
models_dictionary = solve_model(model, 'chem__y_5_set0', models_dictionary, tool_context, description)

3) Plain-language explanation of what changed
- Why acetone is still forced to be present (y[6] = 1):
  - In this model, the “reaction” constraints are logical consistency checks: to set a product y[prod]=0, for every reaction that can produce it there must be at least one missing reactant. Several routes can produce acetone (product 6), notably reaction 6 which uses reactants 11, 12, 13. Here y[11]=1, y[12]=1, y[13]=1 (many are fixed available), so there is a complete route whose reactants are all present. That makes y[6]=0 logically disallowed, hence the model must set y[6]=1 and the objective value remains 1.0.
- What compensates for lack of chemical 5:
  - Reaction 2’s dependence on chemical 5 (hydronium) is no longer usable because y[5]=0, but acetone’s presence is justified through an alternative route (reaction 6) whose reactants are all available. No flows are modeled here—only binary presence/absence—so “compensation” means another feasible route exists with all its reactants present.
- Constraints that are binding after the change (selected highlights):
  - Binding logical constraints (true at equality): reaction_cons for r06→6, r05→11, r12→24, r13→18, r14→21, r15→27, r16→14, r17→32, r18→8, r19→30, r20→13, r21→1, r22→34; the new availability constraint y[5]==0 is binding.
  - Not binding now: reaction_cons for r02→6 (because y[5]=0 makes it slack), r01→4 and r07→15 are also non-binding in this solution.
- Dual prices (marginal values):
  - Not available. This is a binary (MIP) model; standard LP duals aren’t defined. The solver confirms dual suffix is not provided for MIPs. Qualitatively, the newly added y[5]==0 equality is active (tight), and many availability equalities remain tight as well.

4) If infeasible then minimal supply or relaxation
- Not applicable: the modified model is feasible. Minimal positive supply of chemical 5 required to restore feasibility is 0 (no supply required). No relaxation to other parameters is needed.

5) Warnings and modeling caveats about removing a reactant
- Binary logic only: The model uses binary presence (y[c] in {0,1}) and logical consistency constraints; it does not represent reaction stoichiometry, rates, or flows. A product can still be “present” (y=1) without any single route being chosen or operated—only the existence of at least one route with all reactants present blocks setting the product to zero.
- Multiple routes matter: Removing a reactant from one route may have no effect if another route to the product has all reactants available, as seen here for acetone via reaction 6.
- “Binding” in logic ≠ marginal cost: Binding logical equalities don’t imply shadow prices. If marginal values are needed, solve the LP relaxation or adopt a linearized formulation that admits duals.
- Downstream dependencies: Setting a reactant to zero can force certain intermediates to 0 only if every route to those intermediates requires that reactant. If at least one alternative route exists with all reactants available, the product will still be 1 in this logical framework—even if, physically, capacity or kinetics would prevent production. Consider extending the model with quantities/flows if you need material balances and realistic substitutions.

If you want, I can also:
- Lock a specific acetone route (e.g., enforce or forbid reaction 6) to see whether acetone becomes unattainable without chemical 5.
- Produce an LP relaxation and report duals for the availability constraints to give directional marginal information.

Plain-language explanation — why acetone is still produced
- The model is a logical/presence-style formulation (variables are presence/absence), not a detailed flow/stoichiometry LP. In that formulation a product is forbidden only if every route to produce it is blocked.
- Removing chemical 5 disables reaction 2, but acetone still has at least one other route (for example reaction 6) whose reactants are all available (y[11], y[12], y[13] = 1). That alternate route keeps y[6] = 1, so the objective and feasibility remain unchanged.
- In short: reaction 2 is disabled by removing hydronium, but reaction 6 (or other available route) compensates and produces acetone.

Which constraints become binding / non-binding
- The added constraint y[5] == 0 is binding (tight).
- Logical availability constraints that assert presence of certain reactants/routes remain binding where all required inputs are present (several reaction_cons equalities are tight).
- Reaction 2’s producing constraint becomes non-binding in the sense that it cannot be used because y[5]=0, but overall product availability constraints remain satisfied by alternate routes.

Duals / marginal values
- The model is a mixed-integer (logical) formulation; routine LP dual prices are not available for MIP solutions. So we do not have numeric shadow prices for the material constraints in this MIP run.
- If you need marginal price-like information, I can:
  - run an LP relaxation (relax integrality) to obtain duals, or
  - convert the model to a continuous flow/stoichiometric LP that admits economic interpretation (shadow prices).

If the model had become infeasible
- Not applicable here — model stayed feasible. Therefore no minimal positive supply of chemical 5 was required (0 required).

Practical interpretation and recommended follow-up actions
- If your operational meaning of “chemical 5 unavailable” is truly “cannot be used in any route and no alternate route exists physically,” then you should:
  - explicitly disable every reaction that uses chemical 5 (set the corresponding reaction variables to 0), and/or
  - remove or reduce the demand for downstream products that strictly require chemical 5.
- If you want to know whether acetone can be prevented by removing chemical 5 alone, you must ensure all alternative production routes (e.g., reaction 6) are also disabled.
- If you need economic/marginal impacts (which reactants are most valuable), ask me to run the LP relaxation or a flows-based model so I can report duals and marginal values.

Modeling caveats and warnings
- This is a presence/logic model (binary-like). It does not model quantities, stoichiometric coefficients, or capacities. So conclusions about substitution or compensation are “logical” (is a route available?) rather than quantitative (how much acetone can you produce without hydronium).
- Removing a reactant in this framework only prevents routes that explicitly require it; a product remains if any route still has all inputs present.
- For realistic operational decisions (sizing, capacity, substitution rates), extend the model to continuous material balances or add capacity/flow constraints.

- Reaction 8 reactants: y[15]=0, y[16]=0, y[17]=1
- Reaction 9 reactants: y[12]=1, y[18]=0, y[19]=0

Notable binding constraints in the modified model
- Binding (tight) for acetone-related:
  - reaction_cons['02',6] and reaction_cons['06',6] are binding (they actively justify y[6]=0 by lacking at least one required reactant).
- Not binding for acetone-related:
  - reaction_cons['08',6] and reaction_cons['09',6] are not binding (already satisfied slackly by missing reactants).
- Upstream impacts of y[31]=0:
  - reaction_cons['18',8] is binding with y[8]=0 (nacn is a required reactant for making y[8], so without y[31], y[8] drops to 0).
  - reaction_cons['17',32] becomes non-binding (no need to produce y[32]).
- Availability:
  - material_cons_31_override (y[31]==0) is binding; material_cons[31] was deactivated.

Plain-language explanation of changes
- Setting nacn (y[31]) to zero removes it as a reactant for Reaction 18, so y[8] cannot be produced (y[8] goes to 0). Without y[8], Reaction 5 cannot produce y[11], so y[11] becomes 0. Lacking y[11] breaks Reaction 6’s path to acetone, allowing y[6] to drop to 0. With other acetone paths also missing at least one reactant (R2 missing y[4]; R8 missing y[15] and y[16]; R9 missing y[18] and y[19]), the model remains feasible and the objective improves from 1.0 to 0.0.

Actionable specification of the change
- Deactivate constraint: model.material_cons[31].deactivate()
- Add constraint: model.material_cons_31_override = pyo.Constraint(expr=model.y[31] == 0)

Conclusion
- Chemical 31 (nacn) is not directly used in the acetone-producing reactions (2, 6, 8, 9).
- With y[31]=0 enforced, the model is feasible; the optimal solution has y[6]=0 (acetone not producible under these availabilities), and the full y vector is listed above.

  - Reaction 17: uses y[12], y[30], y[31] → produces y[32]
  - Reaction 18: uses y[12], y[30], y[31] → produces y[8]

Reaction 6 (which requires y[11]) thus loses a reactant and cannot produce acetone.

Because every path to acetone is blocked, y[6] = 0 becomes possible and the objective (which minimizes y[6]) drops from 1.0 to 0.0.
- In short: removing nacn had an indirect effect through upstream dependencies, disabling intermediate chemicals that were needed by at least one acetone route, and therefore acetone production becomes impossible — improving the objective to 0.

# Load base model and add duals
model = load_model('chem', models_dictionary)
model_with_duals = add_dual_suffix(model)

# Deactivate the conflicting availability constraint for y[19] (material_cons[19]: y[19] == 0)
model.material_cons[19].deactivate()

# Force the alternative decision: y[18] = 1 and y[19] = 1 (y[17] is already fixed to 1 in material_cons)
if not hasattr(model, 'force_alt'):
    model.force_alt = pyo.ConstraintList()
model.force_alt.clear()
model.force_alt.add(model.y[18] == 1)
model.force_alt.add(model.y[19] == 1)

    model,
    'chem__y_18_set1_y_19_set1',
    models_dictionary,

What I did (WHY-NOT test)
- I deactivated material_cons[19] and forced y[18]=1, y[19]=1 (y[17] is already fixed to 1). Version: chem__y_18_set1_y_19_set1.
- Result: Feasible, optimal, objective unchanged at y[6]=1.

What in the model blocks or discourages y[17]=y[18]=y[19]=1 in the base run
1) Binding availability constraint
- material_cons[17]: y[17] == 1 (binding) makes 17 present.
- material_cons[19]: y[19] == 0 (binding) forbids 19; this is the direct blocker.

2) Reaction logic that limits when y[18] is implied
- Reaction 13 (r='13'): 1 − y[18] ≤ (1 − y[17]) + (1 − y[24]) with rm['13',18,17]=1 and rm['13',18,24]=1.
  - Interpretation: if both y[17]=1 and y[24]=1, then y[18] must be 1. In the base, y[24]=0, so this constraint binds at equality with y[18]=0 and does not require y[18]=1.
  - This is why y[18] naturally settles at 0 in the base (not because it’s costly, just not implied).

3) No objective penalty for 17/18/19 presence
- Objective is obj: MINIMIZE y[6]. No coefficient on y[17], y[18], or y[19]. So there is no cost-based discouragement.
- In the forced run, obj remained 1.0, confirming no cost impact from setting y[18]=1, y[19]=1.

Which constraints become binding or cause trade-offs when all three are present
- In the forced version (chem__y_18_set1_y_19_set1), with y[12]=1 (available), y[18]=1, y[19]=1:
  - reaction_cons['09',6]: 1 − y[6] ≤ (1 − y[12]) + (1 − y[18]) + (1 − y[19]) becomes binding at 0 ≤ 0.
    • Interpretation: with all three reactants present, reaction 09 implies y[6]=1. This tightens consistency but does not worsen the objective here (y[6] was already 1).
  - reaction_cons['10',20]: 1 − y[20] ≤ (1 − y[12]) + (1 − y[18]) + (1 − y[19]) also becomes binding (0 ≤ 0), forcing y[20]=1 under the same logic.
- reaction_cons['13',18] (linking y[18] to y[17] and y[24]) ceases to be binding when y[18] is forced to 1; this confirms y[18] was not implied in the base because y[24]=0.

Bottom line on feasibility and costs
- Infeasibility: No. The only thing that prevents the triple in the base is the hard availability bound y[19]=0.
- Objective impact: None in this data (y[6] stays at 1 with or without 18/19 present).
- Trade-offs: The main “trade-off” is logical: if 12, 18, 19 are present together, reactions 09 and 10 force y[6]=1 and y[20]=1. That tightens the reaction network but doesn’t change cost here.

Minimal changes to allow all three present
- Easiest: Lift the unavailability of chemical 19.
  • Change material_cons[19] so it no longer fixes y[19]=0 (e.g., remove 19 from unavail_c or deactivate model.material_cons[19]).
  • Optionally, set y[19]=1 (add an availability or a forcing constraint).
- To have y[18]=1 implied by the network (instead of forcing it), also ensure y[24]=1 so reaction 13 fires:
  • Reaction 12 implies y[24]=1 when y[9]=1 and y[23]=1 (rm['12',24,9]=1, rm['12',24,23]=1). In the base, y[9]=1 but y[23]=0.
  • So either:
    - Add 23 to available chemicals (make material_cons[23]: y[23] = 1), which will imply y[24]=1 via reaction 12, and then y[18]=1 via reaction 13 (since 17 is already 1), or
    - Directly set y[24]=1 (if you want to bypass reaction 12).
- If you want to test this change gently, you can relax material_cons[19] (and/or add a small penalty for deviations) rather than hard changing availability.

Useful references to the exact components
- Availability constraints:
  • material_cons[17]: y[17] == 1
  • material_cons[19]: y[19] == 0  ← blocker
- Reactions involving 18, 19:
  • r13 → y[18] needs y[17] and y[24]: rm['13',18,17]=1; rm['13',18,24]=1
  • r09 → y[6] needs y[12], y[18], y[19]: rm['09',6,12]=1; rm['09',6,18]=1; rm['09',6,19]=1
  • r10 → y[20] needs y[12], y[18], y[19]: rm['10',20,12]=1; rm['10',20,18]=1; rm['10',20,19]=1
  • r12 → y[24] needs y[9], y[23]: rm['12',24,9]=1; rm['12',24,23]=1

What this means operationally
- The model’s “why-not” is not a cost or feasibility issue; it’s a policy/availability setting: y[19] is declared unavailable.
- Once that is lifted, setting y[18]=1 and y[19]=1 is consistent and does not worsen the acetone objective in this dataset. If you also want y[18]=1 to be deduced (rather than forced), make y[24]=1 via reaction 12 (by supplying y[23]=1).

2026-02-18 00:13:01.872 | INFO     | optichat.tools.callback_tool:check_llm_response:827 - [Callback] Inspecting LLM response from 'root_agent': [WHY_NOT]

Short answer (plain language)
- The model doesn’t allow y[17], y[18], and y[19] to all appear together in the base solution because chemical 19 is hard-set unavailable (material_cons[19] fixes y[19] = 0). That single availability constraint is the direct blocker. y[18] is also not automatically present because the reaction that would imply it needs y[24] as well, and y[24] is 0 in the base run.
- There is no objective cost preventing them: the objective only minimizes y[6] (acetone). Turning y[17], y[18], y[19] on does not by itself increase the objective in the current data.

Evidence from the model (specific constraints / reactions)
- Availability (material) constraints:
  - material_cons[17]: y[17] == 1 (makes 17 present).
  - material_cons[19]: y[19] == 0 (explicitly forbids 19 — this is the direct reason the triple cannot be present).
- Reaction logic connecting them:
  - Reaction 13 → y[18] requires y[17] and y[24]:
    - Constraint form: 1 − y[18] ≤ (1 − y[17]) + (1 − y[24])
    - So y[18] becomes 1 only if both y[17]=1 and y[24]=1. In the base, y[24]=0, so y[18] is not implied.
  - Reaction 9 → y[6] needs y[12], y[18], y[19]:
    - rm entries: rm['09',6,12]=1; rm['09',6,18]=1; rm['09',6,19]=1.
    - If 12, 18, 19 are all present, that reaction would force y[6]=1.
  - Reaction 10 → y[20] similarly needs y[12], y[18], y[19], so those reactions become active if 18 & 19 are present.
  - Reaction 12 → y[24] needs y[9] and y[23]. Since y[23]=0 in the base, y[24] remains 0.

What this implies (trade-offs / bindings)
- The key “why-not” is not a cost trade-off but a hard availability setting: y[19] is fixed to 0. Remove that and you can have all three present without making the model infeasible or increasing the objective in the given dataset.
- If you turn y[18] and y[19] on (or lift material_cons[19]), some reaction implications become binding:
  - r09 and r10 will force y[6]=1 and y[20]=1 if their reactants are present.
  - r13’s implication of y[18] will be satisfied if y[24] is also present; otherwise y[18] may need to be forced explicitly.
- There is no hidden penalty term discouraging y[17]/y[18]/y[19]; the network logic and explicit availability settings determine presence.

Minimal changes to allow all three simultaneously
- Easiest and direct:
  - Remove or modify the availability constraint that forbids chemical 19:
    - Deactivate material_cons[19] or change it so y[19] is allowed (e.g., material_cons[19]: y[19] == 1 or remove the constraint entirely).
- If you want y[18] to become implied (not forced) by the network rather than manually setting it:
  - Ensure y[24] = 1 so that reaction 13 implies y[18] when y[17]=1.
    - To make y[24] = 1 via existing reactions, enable the inputs to reaction 12: set y[23] = 1 (since r12 needs y[9] and y[23]).
    - Or directly set y[24] = 1 (add a forcing constraint).
- Testing approach (practical steps to try in the model):
  1. Deactivate material_cons[19] and re-solve: this will show if y[19]=1 can be chosen by the solver (or force y[19]=1 directly and re-solve).
  2. If you prefer y[18] to be deduced: set y[23]=1, re-solve, and check whether r12 → y[24]=1 then r13 → y[18]=1.
  3. Optionally run the LP relaxation if you want marginal information (duals) about these changes.

Modeling caveats
- This is a logical/presence-style model: the implications are logical (if reactants present then product must be present). There is no cost coefficient on y[17], y[18], y[19], so their presence is driven by availability constraints and logical connectivity rather than objective trade-offs.
- For operational/quantity-based decisions (how much of each chemical to stock), convert or extend the model to continuous flows and stoichiometry so you can assess capacity, volumes, and marginal costs.

If you want, I can:
- Make the change (deactivate material_cons[19]), re-run the model and show the new solution so you can see the precise implications; or
- Test the sequence to make y[18] implied (set y[23]=1 → y[24]=1 → y[18]=1) and report the solution and any new bindings. Which would you like me to run?

from pyomo.environ import value

# Load base model
model = load_model('chem', models_dictionary)

# Add the new at-most-one constraint: y[1] + y[2] + y[3] <= 1
if hasattr(model, 'at_most_one_1_2_3'):
    model.del_component(model.at_most_one_1_2_3)
model.at_most_one_1_2_3 = pyo.Constraint(expr=model.y[1] + model.y[2] + model.y[3] <= 1)

# Solve and store under a new version name
version_name = 'chem__y_1_2_3_le1'
description = (
    'What-if analysis: Added a new constraint enforcing at most one among chemicals 1, 2, and 3 '
    '(y[1]+y[2]+y[3] <= 1) while keeping all base availability constraints. This tests whether '
    'acetone (y[6]) can still be produced when only one of the first three chemicals may be present '
    'given the original raw-material/catalyst availability.'
)
models_dictionary = solve_model(model, version_name, models_dictionary, tool_context, description)

# Summarize feasibility and, if solved to optimality, collect y values
result = {
    'version': version_name,
    'status': models_dictionary[version_name]['solve_status'],
    'objective': None,
    'y': None
}
if models_dictionary[version_name]['solve_status'].lower() == 'optimal':
    result['objective'] = value(model.obj)
    result['y'] = {i: int(round(value(model.y[i]))) for i in model.C}

result

Short answer

- Therefore you cannot get a feasible solution (and there is no computed value for acetone y[6]) unless you relax the cap or change the base availabilities that force the contradiction.

Exactly what I changed (so you can reproduce)
- Added constraint: at_most_one_1_2_3 : y[1] + y[2] + y[3] <= 1
- Kept all other base availability constraints unchanged (so material_cons[2] and material_cons[3], which set y[2]=1 and y[3]=1, remain active).

Why it is infeasible (plain language)
- The infeasibility is structural and immediate: base data forces both y[2] and y[3] to equal 1. The new rule allows at most one of them. That yields 2 ≤ 1, which is impossible. The solver cannot produce a feasible assignment for any y[1..34] under that combined set of constraints.

Minimal fixes (numeric and practical)
- Minimal numeric relaxation: change the new cap RHS from 1 to 2:
  - y[1] + y[2] + y[3] <= 2
  - This is the smallest numeric change that makes the constraint consistent with the base (it allows the base y[2]=1, y[3]=1).
- Alternative practical fixes while keeping the <=1 policy:
  - Turn off one of the currently-required chemicals by changing availability:
    - Set y[2] = 0 (deactivate material_cons[2]) OR
    - Set y[3] = 0 (deactivate material_cons[3]).
  - Either option yields y[1]+y[2]+y[3] ≤ 1 and restores feasibility.
  - Note: y[1] is 0 in the base solution, so changing y[1] does not resolve the contradiction.

- Yes. In the base model acetone is produced via reaction r06 (requires y[11], y[12], y[13]) and that path does NOT depend on chemicals 1, 2, or 3. So after you restore feasibility (by one of the minimal fixes above), acetone production can still be achieved using r06—you do not need chemicals 1–3 for the operative acetone route in the current data.
- If you remove one of y[2] or y[3] (as suggested above), acetone remains producible by r06 and the optimal objective is unaffected in practice.

# Inspect base model data and solution, and the infeasible variant's conflicting fixed variables
base = load_model('chem', models_dictionary)

# Gather availability info
avail_c = base.component('material_cons')._body() if False else None  # placeholder; we'll access data dict via model attributes

# The 'avail_c' and 'unavail_c' were captured when building the model in global 'data'.
# They are not direct components, but we can retrieve them from the model attributes we know were captured at build time.
try:
    avail_c = base.avail_c
except AttributeError:
    # Attach for inspection by reconstructing from Param 'y' fixations in material_cons
    avail_c = []
    unavail_c = []
    for c in base.C:
        con = base.material_cons[c]
        if con.body.polynomial_degree() == 0:
            # It is an equality y[c] == const; check const via bounds on var
            lb = base.y[c].lb
            ub = base.y[c].ub
            if lb == 1 and ub == 1:
                avail_c.append(c)
            elif lb == 0 and ub == 0:
                unavail_c.append(c)
    base.avail_c = avail_c
    base.unavail_c = unavail_c

# Also try to directly get 'unavail_c' if not set
unavail_c = getattr(base, 'unavail_c', [])

# Extract solution values for base
base_vals = {int(c): int(round(base.y[c]())) for c in base.C}

# Examine the reaction IDs and reactants for acetone product (c=6) in reactions 2,6,8,9
R_list = list(base.R)
# Identify exact matching reaction keys for 2,6,8,9 (which may be strings like '02')
# We'll match by numeric value if possible

# Build a mapping from reaction key to an integer if key is like '2' or '02', else keep as-is
rxn_map = {}
for r in base.R:
    try:
        rxn_map[int(str(r))] = r
    except Exception:
        # Try strip non-digits
        import re
        digits = ''.join(re.findall(r'\d+', str(r)))
        if digits:
            rxn_map[int(digits)] = r

acetone_rxns_keys = []
for k in [2,6,8,9]:
    if k in rxn_map:
        acetone_rxns_keys.append(rxn_map[k])

# Collect reactants per these reactions for product 6
rm = base.rm
acetone_reactants = {}
for r in acetone_rxns_keys:
    reac = [int(c) for c in base.C if (r, 6, c) in rm and int(rm[r,6,c]) == 1]
    acetone_reactants[r] = sorted(reac)

# Count which of y[3],y[4],y[5] are fixed to 1 in base (via material_cons)
forced_ones = []
forced_zeros = []
for c in [3,4,5]:
    lb = base.y[c].lb
    ub = base.y[c].ub
    if lb == 1 and ub == 1:
        forced_ones.append(c)
    elif lb == 0 and ub == 0:
        forced_zeros.append(c)

result = {
    'avail_c': avail_c,
    'unavail_c': unavail_c,
    'base_objective': float(base.obj()),
    'base_y': base_vals,
    'rxn_ids_in_model': R_list,
    'acetone_rxns_found': acetone_rxns_keys,
    'acetone_reactants': acetone_reactants,
    'forced_ones_345': forced_ones,
    'forced_zeros_345': forced_zeros,
}

# Extract base solution values and identify y[3], y[4], y[5]
base = load_model('chem', models_dictionary)

base_vals = {}
for c in list(base.y):
    base_vals[int(c)] = int(round(base.y[c].value))

triplet_vals = {k: base_vals.get(k, None) for k in [3,4,5]}

# Also get acetone y[6]
acetone_val = base_vals.get(6, None)

# Identify reaction IDs 2,6,8,9 within model.R (keys may be strings like '02')
rxn_ids = {}
for r in base.R:
    s = str(r)
    try:
        num = int(s)
    except:
        import re
        m = re.findall(r'\d+', s)
        num = int(m[0]) if m else None
    if num is not None and num in [2,6,8,9]:
        rxn_ids[num] = r

# Gather reactants that map to product 6 (acetone) for these reactions
acetone_reactants = {}
if hasattr(base, 'rm'):
    for num in [2,6,8,9]:
        r_key = rxn_ids.get(num, None)
        if r_key is None:
            continue
        reactants = []
        for c in base.C:
            try:
                if (r_key, 6, c) in base.rm and int(base.rm[r_key, 6, c]) == 1:
                    reactants.append(int(c))
            except:
                pass
        acetone_reactants[num] = sorted(reactants)

# For the infeasible variant, compute how many of y3,y4,y5 are forced to 1 via material_cons by checking equality constraints bodies numerically at base solution
forced_ones_345 = []
forced_zeros_345 = []
for k in [3,4,5]:
    con = base.material_cons[k]
    # Check if equality to 1 or 0 by probing y[k]=0 and y[k]=1 and seeing residual on body
    # But simpler: check if the constraint is of form y[k] == 1 or y[k] == 0 using the constant term heuristic
    body = con.body
    # Evaluate body at y[k]=0 and y[k]=1 by temporarily setting value
    old = base.y[k].value
    base.y[k].value = 0
    val0 = float(body())
    base.y[k].value = 1
    val1 = float(body())
    base.y[k].value = old
    # For equality body == 0: if body is y[k]-1: val0=-1, val1=0  => forced to 1. If body is y[k]-0: val0=0, val1=1 => forced to 0
    if abs(val0 + 1) < 1e-8 and abs(val1 - 0) < 1e-8:
        forced_ones_345.append(k)
    elif abs(val0 - 0) < 1e-8 and abs(val1 - 1) < 1e-8:
        forced_zeros_345.append(k)

result = {
    'base_objective': float(base.obj()),
    'base_y_3_4_5': triplet_vals,
    'acetone_y6': acetone_val,
    'acetone_reactants': acetone_reactants,
    'forced_ones_345': forced_ones_345,
    'forced_zeros_345': forced_zeros_345,
}
result
